The 2022 budget aimed to stabilize debt and deficits; PEMEX continued to report losses and high financial obligations and remained dependent on substantial government support.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.6", "5B.9", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.6"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The evidence concerns 2019–2022 and forecasts 2023; it is contextual rather than a direct 2023 execution finding.", "ruler_attribution": "The fiscal choices and PEMEX support are attributed to the government during López Obrador's administration.", "limitations": ["The document's 2023 figures are projections made in 2022, not observed 2023 outcomes."], "start_unit": 18, "end_unit": 20, "locator": "page 18 through page 20", "exact_excerpt": "The World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nThe average return on equity increased to 14.5 percent in 2021, after a sharp decline to 5.2 percent in\n2020 due to higher provisions.\n13. The authorities' countercyclical fiscal response remains cautious with public sector borrowing\nrequirements (PSBR) of 3.8 percent of GDP during 2021. Direct budgetary support in 2020 amounted to\n0.7 percent of GDP whereas guarantees and loans to MSMEs by development banks amounted to 1.2\npercent of GDP, focused on increased public health spending, small credits to MSMEs and the informal\nsector, and targeted social transfers to vulnerable groups. Mexico's overall PSBR were 4 percent of GDP\nin 2020, driven by a moderate increase in public spending and sustained revenues fueled by strong tax\nadministration measures and tax settlements with large companies. Yet, net public debt as a share of GDP\nincreased to 52.4 percent due to the fiscal deficit, debt reevaluation determined by foreign exchange\n(FOREX), and the large contraction in nominal GDP in 2020. The fiscal response during the pandemic, albeit\nat the risk of a deeper contraction and the sharp depreciation led to an increase in public indebtedness,\nbelow however to what was observed in other emerging markets. The 2021 fiscal situation benefited from\nthe economic recovery and higher oil prices, an expenditure that continues part of the pandemic fiscal\nstimulus centered on public health and social transfers and implementing large public investment\nprojects.\n14. Petróleos Mexicanos (PEMEX) has continued to report losses, equivalent to MXN 224 billion\n(US$10.9 billion) in 2021. Financial support from the Government during 2019–2021 (amounting to a\ncumulative 2.7 percent of 2021 GDP) and higher oil prices mitigated these losses. Federal government\nsupport to PEMEX came in the form of tax relief (US$12.7 billion), financing debt repayments (US$10\nbillion), and direct funding for investment projects (US$6.5 billion), and cash support for other obligations\n(US$6.7 billion), totaling US$35.9 billion (2.7 percent of GDP)18. This financial support was aimed to\npartially compensate for the US$41.6 billion losses of 2019 and 2020. PEMEX’s financial debt remains high\nand total financial obligations reached MXN 2243 billion (US$109 billion) at the end of 2021. PEMEX is\nexpected to benefit from the higher oil prices as the Mexican-Mix oil price averaged US$35.7 per barrel in\n2020, to reach US$64.7 in 2021, while averaging almost US$90 per barrel in the first quarter of 2022.\nPEMEX's oil production declined from an average of over 2 million barrels per day during 2015-2017, to\naround 1.75 million barrels per day in 2021. The fiscal support improves some of PEMEX challenges related\nto significant tax burden and high financial cost. A turnaround of PEMEX finances would also require to\nprioritize profitable business areas by redirecting investment for replacing reserves, scaling down large\ninvestment projects and focusing on maintaining and upgrading existing refineries, stimulating synergies\nwith the private sector in exploration and oil production, and selling noncore assets. PEMEX remains three\nnotches below investment grade for Fitch and Moody’s after a downgrade by Moody’s by one notch in\nJuly 2021 (it is still investment grade for S&P). The fiscal support to PEMEX as well as its balance and debt\nare part of the fiscal account figures reported for the consolidated public sector (tables 1 and 2).\n15. The 2022 public budget approved aims to stabilize the overall fiscal deficit at 2.7 percent of GDP\nand stabilize the net public debt-to-GDP ratio at 49.6 percent over the medium term.19 On the\n18 Box 2. IMF, Mexico Article IV, November 2021.\n19 Considering the most updated fiscal scenario, Precriterios de política económica para el 2023.\nPage 14\n\nThe World Bank\nMexico Inclusive and Sustainable Economic Growth DPL (P178224)\nexpenditure side, the budget envisions a re-prioritization of expenditures toward health (a 27.6 percent\nincrease in real terms compared to 2021) and social assistance programs (real increment of 12.8 percent)\nto vulnerable groups while keeping public investment at 3.1 percent of GDP, mainly for flagship projects.\nThe impact of the pandemic-induced economic downturn in 2020 on debt is not expected to affect the\ndebt consolidation process, based on a contained fiscal deficit under the long-standing prudent fiscal\npolicy of Mexico, further supported by the limited fiscal impulse to remove. The budget envisages\nincreases in public revenues driven by the recovery in economic activity and efforts to tackle elusion and\nevasion. Also, to simplify the tax filing process, reducing costs, and increasing the tax base, the new budget\nlaw adopts (a) a simplified tax filing regime for independent workers that limits deductions and (b) a\nspecial regime for small firms with accelerated depreciation to boost investment and employment.\nTable 1. Growth is expected to be\n3.2 percent in 2023, led by robust private consumption and investment, with notable strength\nin services and construction sector, and in auto production. The unemployment rate has fallen\nto 2.7 percent. Proactive monetary policy and a decline in global commodity prices are\nfacilitating disinflation. Economic activity is projected to slow to 2.1 percent in 2024. Although\nfiscal policy is expected to loosen next year, its impact on growth will be blunted by binding\ncapacity constraints, a continuation of tight monetary policy, and slowing growth in the U.S.\n\nThe authorities are projected to meet their 2023 fiscal targets. More restrained capital\nspending is expected to more than offset the lower tax revenue, especially on the VAT,\nyielding an overall deficit of 3.9 percent of GDP. This should result in a decline of gross public\nsector debt (by staff’s definition) to 52.7 percent of GDP in 2023. Banxico has held its policy\nrate at 11.25 percent since March 2023 and stated it will keep rate on hold for an extended\nperiod. With inflation and long-run inflation expectations in check, the real rate is now firmly in\ncontractionary territory.\n\nThe banking sector has strong capital positions and, as of May 2023, nonperforming loans are\nclose to record lows at 2.2 percent of total loans. Higher freight costs, strong domestic\ndemand, and adverse price developments increased the current account deficit slightly in\n2022 to 1.2 percent of GDP. Higher demand-driven imports are expected to widen the deficit\nfurther in 2023 to 1.5 percent of GDP. International reserves remain at comfortable levels.\n\nExecutive Board Assessment2\n\nExecutive Directors agreed with the thrust of the staff appraisal. They noted that the\nauthorities’ very strong policies and policy frameworks were instrumental in restraining public\ndebt and containing inflation, while achieving a broad-based economic expansion supported\nby robust private consumption and investment. Recognizing the opportunities for Mexico over\nthe medium term, Directors noted that securing sustainable and inclusive growth will require\ncontinuing with a sound macroeconomic policy mix, accompanied by a broad set of structural\nreforms to address the existing bottlenecks and make the economy more climate resilient.\n\n\n\n\n1 Under Article IV of the IMF's Articles of Agreement, the IMF holds bilateral discussions with members, usually every year. A staff\nteam visits the country, collects economic and financial information, and discusses with officials the country's economic developments\nand policies. On return to headquarters, the staff prepares a report, which forms the basis for discussion by the Executive Board.\n2 At the conclusion of the discussion, the Managing Director, as Chairman of the Board, summarizes the views of Executive Directors,\nand this summary is transmitted to the country's authorities. An explanation of any qualifiers used in summings up can be found here:\nhttp://www.IMF.org/external/np/sec/misc/qualifiers.htm.\n\nDirectors generally cautioned against an overly procyclical near-term fiscal stance and\nunderscored that decisive measures will be needed in 2025 and beyond to preserve fiscal\nsustainability over the medium term, while highlighting Mexico’s strong track record of meeting\nfiscal targets. Going forward, Directors emphasized the need to boost non-oil revenues, which\nremain below Latin American and OECD peers, noting that higher fiscal space will create\nroom for targeted social and infrastructure spending. They also saw scope to reform the\nmedium-term fiscal framework to increase its flexibility and credibility. Directors agreed that\ngreater transparency in fiscal reporting will improve accountability and welcomed the\ntransparent recording of support to Pemex in 2024 budget, while emphasizing the importance\nof ensuring the company’s commercial viability.\n\nDirectors agreed that the proactive approach of Banco de México to monetary policy has been\ninstrumental in containing inflationary pressures and ensuring that inflation expectations\nremain well-anchored. In the face of upside risks to inflation, they recommended caution in\nreducing the policy rate before inflation is on a clearer downward path toward the target.\nDirectors also underscored the importance of continuing to enhance communication practices.\nThey agreed that the flexible exchange rate should continue to be the key tool to facilitate\nadjustment to external and domestic shocks.\n\nDirectors agreed that the financial system remains resilient, with high capital and liquidity\nbuffers. They looked forward to continued implementation of key policy recommendations of\nthe 2022 Financial Sector Assessment Program. Directors emphasized the need to address\noutstanding gaps in the AML/CFT framework and enhance collaboration between various\nAML/CFT agencies and anti-corruption bodies. They also welcomed Mexico volunteering for\nan assessment of the transnational aspects of corruption.\n\nDirectors underscored the importance of supply side reforms to improve potential growth and\nraise living standards, including by taking advantage of the diversification of global supply\nchains. Given the significant gender gaps, they emphasized that these reforms should\ncomprise policies to further boost female labor force participation and remove legal\nimpediments to female economic empowerment. Better tackling corruption and crime,\nexpanding financial inclusion, as well as improving infrastructure and streamlining regulations\nwill also be key.\n\nDirectors agreed that a comprehensive and well-sequenced climate change strategy can\nprovide more durable sources of energy. Given the long-term risk of a reduction in global\ndemand for hydrocarbons, they encouraged switching to low carbon and renewable sources of\ngeneration, including by considering increasing the price of carbon.\n\nIt is expected that the next Article IV Consultation with Mexico will be held on the standard 12-\nmonth cycle.", "excerpt_sha256": "50ae24657b1c8e1e1ea7c12a121b82b78cacdf1f9c1417d147f84db074c960fa", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E001", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "The Mexican authorities used largely untargeted subsidies to mitigate cost-of-living pressures. Headline inflation rose from about 3 percent at the end-2020 to 7.4\npercent by end-2021 and to 8.7 percent by August this year. Economic activity is expected to\nslow from 2.1 percent in 2022 to 1.2 percent in 2023, given weaker U.S. and global growth\nand tighter global financial conditions. Inflation is projected to plateau in the second half of\n2022 and then gradually decline.\n\nThe government has maintained a broadly neutral fiscal stance in 2022, targeting a fiscal\ndeficit of 3.8 percent of GDP. It has used largely untargeted subsidies to mitigate the rise in\nthe cost of living, offsetting the windfall gains from oil revenues. The gross debt of the public\nsector (by staff’s definition) is estimated at about 56 percent of GDP. With inflation above\ntarget, the central bank has increased the policy rate to 9.25 percent in a series of hikes.\nReflecting more resilient domestic demand growth compared to output, the current account\nposted a deficit of 0.4 percent of GDP in 2021 and is expected to widen to around 1 percent of\nGDP in 2022-2023. International reserves remain at a comfortable level.\n\nThe Mexican banking system remains well-capitalized, and its profitability continues to recover\nfrom the impact of the pandemic. Risk analysis as part of the Financial Sector Assessment\nProgram (FSAP), conducted in parallel with the Article IV consultation, found that the systemic\nvulnerabilities and system-wide liquidity risks in the financial system remain broadly contained,\ngiven high capital buffers, low private sector leverage, and no sign of stretched asset prices.\nHowever, tail risks bear close monitoring. Financial sector policies have been strengthened in\nrecent years, but further steps are necessary to maintain the resilience of the financial system\nunder the evolving risk environment.\n\nExecutive Board Assessment2\n\nExecutive Directors agreed with the thrust of the staff appraisal. They welcomed the\nauthorities’ prudent macroeconomic policies in the face of a surge in inflation. They noted that\neconomic growth is expected to slow in the near term reflecting weaker U.S. growth and\ntighter global financial conditions. With increased risks in a more turbulent global environment,\n\n\n\n1 Under Article IV of the IMF's Articles of Agreement, the IMF holds bilateral discussions with members, usually every year. A staff\nteam visits the country, collects economic and financial information, and discusses with officials the country's economic developments\nand policies. On return to headquarters, the staff prepares a report, which forms the basis for discussion by the Executive Board.\n2 At the conclusion of the discussion, the Managing Director, as Chairman of the Board, summarizes the views of Executive Directors,\nand this summary is transmitted to the country's authorities. An explanation of any qualifiers used in summings up can be found here:\nhttp://www.IMF.org/external/np/sec/misc/qualifiers.htm.\n\n2\n\n\nDirectors agreed that Mexico is well placed to navigate the challenges, owing to its very strong\nmacroeconomic policies and policy frameworks.\n\nDirectors welcomed Banco de México’s proactive approach to addressing the inflation surge.\nWith significant uncertainty about the inflation path in 2023 and upside risks, they concurred\nthat further increases in the policy rate and maintaining a restrictive policy stance may be\nneeded for some time. They also generally underscored the importance of clear policy\ncommunication. Directors recommended aligning wage increases more closely with expected\ninflation and the productivity of lower wage workers.\n\nDirectors agreed that the envisaged neutral fiscal stance in 2022 and 2023 is appropriate.\nThey noted that retail fuel price smoothing, while reducing cost pressures, had a sizable cost\non the budget, and generally encouraged the authorities to shift toward more targeted support\nshould oil prices rise again in order to protect other priority spending. Contingency plans\nshould be prepared for a swift policy response should downside risks materialize. Increasing\nfiscal buffers in the short term and reforming the institutional framework for greater flexibility in\nthe longer term would be important. Directors noted that the floating exchange rate should\ncontinue to act as a shock absorber, although foreign exchange intervention could be\nconsidered if there is a significant worsening of market illiquidity.\n\nDirectors agreed that the financial system remains resilient post pandemic, welcomed the\nfavorable assessment of the effectiveness of Mexico’s financial stability framework, and\nbroadly supported the key policy recommendations of the 2022 Financial Sector Assessment\nProgram. They concurred that additional measures, including an update of the financial sector\noversight and crisis management frameworks, are needed to keep up with a changing risk and\nregulatory landscape. After aligning the legal and regulatory AML/CFT framework with\nFinancial Action Task Force standards, the focus should now be to strengthen the\neffectiveness of the AML/CFT regime, including through adequate resources, ensuring\navailability of high-quality beneficial ownership information, and monitoring emerging financial\nrisks related to fintech.\n\nDirectors urged the authorities to broaden the supply side policy agenda for higher and more\ninclusive growth. Efforts should include measures to address corruption and crime and\nstrengthen the rule of law, increase human capital investment, tackle infrastructure\nbottlenecks, reduce labor and product market rigidities, and promote financial deepening and\ninclusion. Directors recommended a gradual increase in productive spending, financed by tax\nreforms, to improve human and physical capital.\n\nDirectors generally noted the need to take further steps toward reducing greenhouse\nemissions. Fostering greater private sector participation in the energy sector would help boost\ncompetitiveness and investment.\n\nIt is expected that the next Article IV consultation with Mexico will be held on the standard 12-\nmonth cycle.", "excerpt_sha256": "cbe620a4c52802d4d0990b77e14d0884d669e805b8c7969ff431d01436d0f522", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports largely untargeted subsidies and the fiscal-cost/targeting recommendation.", "The excerpt does not support attributing disproportionate benefits to higher-income households; IMF staff judged the response appropriately calibrated but noted significant uncertainty and persistent upside risks.", "question_ids": ["5B.9", "8B.9"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "The evidence concerns policy decisions through September-November 2022 and expected continuation into 2023; it is relevant to the 2023 crisis-management and macroeconomic-policy setting.", "ruler_attribution": "The report attributes monetary decisions to Banco de México and the authorities; institutional central-bank autonomy limits direct attribution to AMLO.", "limitations": ["The source does not establish whether AMLO personally directed or interfered with monetary policy.", "Reported inflation outcomes for 2023 are projections, not observed results."], "start_unit": 17, "end_unit": 20, "locator": "page 17 through page 20", "exact_excerpt": "MEXICO\n\n\n\nPOLICY DISCUSSIONS\nA. Tackling High Inflation\n\nMonetary Policy\n\n19.      Banxico has taken a proactive approach to address increasingly broad-based inflation.\nSince June 2021, successive and gradually larger increases in the reference rate brought the ex ante\nreal policy rate (i.e., the nominal rate adjusted for one-year ahead inflation expectations) to a\nmoderately restrictive level by September 2022 at about 4 percent. 6 This has been an appropriately\ncalibrated response to the upward surprises to inflation and sequential delays in returning to the\ninflation target. In recent monetary policy statements, Banxico has also rightly indicated its intention\nto further increase the policy rate. It is expected that a policy rate of at least 10 percent would be\nrequired for inflation to reverse close to the central bank’s 3-percent target within the usual two\nyears or so.\n\n\n                   Inflation and Expectations\n                                                                        Real Ex-Ante Policy Rates\n                    (Seasonally adjusted, y/y)\n         9                                                                      (Percent)\n                                                                 10\n                                                                        Brazil\n                                                  Headline\n         8                                        Inflation      8      Chile\n                                                                        Mexico\n         7                                        Core           6      Peru\n                                                  Inflation\n                                                                        Colombia\n                                                                 4\n         6                                        Expectations\n                                                  : 12 Months    2\n         5                                        Expectations\n                                                                 0\n                                                  : 1-4 Years\n         4                                                       -2\n                                                  Expectations\n                                                  : 5-8 Years\n         3                                                       -4\n\n\n\n\n        Sources: Banxico and Haver Analytics.\n\n\n\n20.      Inflation risks are expected to remain heightened for some time. The proactive policy\ntightening already put in place, alongside further increases in the policy rate, and weakening global\ndemand are expected to lead to a slowdown in activity and a decline in inflation. However, there is\nsignificant uncertainty about the timing, speed, and durability of the downward path for inflation.\nThis uncertainty arises from the possibility of: (i) further shocks, including spillovers to local raw and\nprocessed food prices from global commodity prices and domestic food prices, especially resulting\nfrom severe drought conditions in some Mexican states; (ii) if inflation imported from advanced\neconomies is more persistent than expected; (iii) longer-lasting price pressures from supply chain\n\n\n6 In its April–June 2019 Quarterly Report, Banxico used a range of models to establish a range between 1.8 and 3.4\n\npercent for the neutral real policy rate.\n\n\n\n                                                                           INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND           13\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nconstraints; (iv) further expected increases in the minimum wage feeding into costs and prices;\n(v) inertia resulting from more frequent domestic price adjustments; and (vi) further upticks in near-\nterm inflation expectations that then feed in to wages and prices. The inflation surge in Mexico\nreflects both shocks in standard determinants (such as relative food and import prices, falling slack,\nand rising inflation expectations), but also a series of positive residual factors, and preliminary signs\nof greater inertia (Annex IX).\n\n\n                Inflation Expectations—1-4 Years Ahead                           Mexico Processed Food and U.S. Overall Food\n                      (Central Bank survey responses)                                             Inflation\n     6.0                                                              0.5   14\n                                                                                                              Mex - Core Food\n                                                                            12\n                                                                      0.4                                     U.S. - Food & Bev\n     5.0\n                                                                            10\n                                                                      0.3\n                                                                             8\n     4.0\n                                                                      0.2    6\n\n     3.0                                                                     4\n                                                                      0.1\n                                                                             2\n     2.0                                                              0.0\n                                                                             0\n\n                                                                            -2\n              Interquartile Range        Total Range\n              Average                    Interquartile Spread (RHS)\n\n\n Sources: Haver Analytics and Banxico.\n\n\n\n21.      Risk considerations call for further increases in the policy rate and maintaining it at\nthat higher level. The risks to inflation are not only high but also asymmetric in the usual one-to-\ntwo-year monetary policy horizon: upside surprises to inflation seem more likely than downside\nshocks. One contributing factor is backward-looking elements in the inflation process in Mexico\naccording to Philips curve estimates for Mexico (Annex IX), which suggests additional risks from\nrecent inflation shocks. Given these asymmetric risks and the substantial costs of de-anchoring wage\nand price formation, a risk management approach would argue for policy rates to rise further and\nstay firmly restrictive for some time, so as to keep wage and price formation anchored. Such an\napproach would entail a real ex ante policy rate that would peak at over 5 percent in 2023, broadly\nconsistent with market expectations for nominal rates and staff’s inflation forecast.\n\n22.     Clear monetary policy communication will increase policy effectiveness during this\nperiod of significant inflation uncertainty. Banxico has appropriately taken steps to help the\npublic better understand its policy decisions in this context. Steps have included the publication of\nupdated inflation forecasts with every rate decision since August 2021 and, more recently, guidance\non the direction of the next policy rate change in the monetary policy statement. These efforts have\nhelped the public better understand the Governing Board’s policy decision. To further strengthen\nthis understanding, Banxico could begin publishing more information on the policy rate path that\nunderpins its macro forecast, including the expected rate and length of its stay at the peak of the\ntightening cycle, and possible triggers for changes in policies. If clearly presented as expectations\nand not a policy commitment, changes in the expected policy rate path would provide valuable\ninformation on how the central bank expects to adjust policy as economic conditions change. A\nbroader review of the experience with Banxico’s inflation targeting framework over the past two\n\n\n\n14         INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ndecades could provide useful suggestions for further improvements to the policy framework and the\ncommunications toolkit.\n\nFiscal Measures\n\n23.      The authorities have used largely untargeted subsidies to mitigate the rise in the cost\nof living. The authorities expect that these subsidies will cost around 2 percent of GDP (see Annex\nVI for a description of the authorities’ anti-inflationary fiscal measures) which include:\n\n•   Retail fuel price stabilization has directly reduced cost pressures, lowering inflation by an\n    estimated 2 percentage points this year. The measure has come at a sizeable budgetary cost,\n    estimated at around 1.5 percent of GDP, and has disproportionately benefited higher income\n    households. Furthermore, by diluting price signals, the policy has short-circuited the desirable\n    adjustment in fuel demand.\n\n•   The measures to mitigate the impact of higher food prices have come at a smaller budgetary\n    cost. They have mostly relied on preexisting programs (e.g., increased emphasis on direct\n    fertilizer provision to small farmers or support for subsistence farming), and their direct impact\n    on inflation has been minimal.\n\n24.      Overall, these fiscal and preexisting redistributive measures have helped support real\nincomes. The higher food and energy prices have only increased the deficit by around ¼ percent of\nGDP with fuel subsidies offset by higher oil revenues and food-related measures funded from\nreducing other expenditures. However, in the event of a renewed increase in energy prices, the fiscal\neffects would depend on the level of refining margins (“crack spread”) and the duration of the\nhigher global prices. In addition to these efforts to lessen the increase in the cost of living, the\nadministration has increased the universal social (noncontributory) pensions and is projected to\nraise the minimum wage from 42 percent of the median formal sector wage in 2018 to 59 percent in\n2022). Overall, in the context of a stronger economic recovery and record-high remittance inflow,\nthese fiscal efforts, the higher minimum wage, and other labor market measures may have\ncontributed to an increase in real per capita labor income of about 5 percent (y/y) by mid-2022.\n\n25.      The proposed neutral fiscal stance in 2023 strikes a balance between supporting\nmonetary policy in its disinflation efforts while avoiding a material fiscal drag on activity.\nWith the economy currently operating at close to potential the priority is to restore low and stable\ninflation. A restrictive monetary stance and a broadly neutral fiscal stance provide an appropriate\npolicy mix to achieve this goal. However, continuing large minimum wage increases will likely add to\ninflation pressures, working at counter purpose to monetary and fiscal policies and potentially\nreducing formal employment for lower income workers (see below).\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n26.    The authorities agreed with the need for a robust monetary policy to keep inflation\nexpectations well-anchored. The early and decisive monetary policy response to the global\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       15\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\ninflation surge had been successful, noting that upside risks to inflation were recognized early even\nwhen the surge was still considered transitory in the baseline. They underscored that the real policy\nrate was now in restrictive territory despite continued upward surprises to inflation. The authorities\nhighlighted their strengthened communication efforts, which had ensured smooth financial market\nadjustment in this hiking cycle. They will continue to evaluate other tools that could improve\ncommunication.\n\n27.      The anti-inflationary fiscal measures have helped to mitigate the effects of inflation on\nthose most exposed. The authorities emphasized that, despite these measures, they sustained\nmomentum on the administration’s flagship reforms. Fuel price stabilization in particular had been\ncentral to efforts to support households and restrain inflation, in line with the President’s\ncommitment to the people. They highlighted analysis suggesting that, in addition to supporting\nhouseholds, the pricing mechanism has dampening second-round inflation effects. Other anti-\ninflation measures had largely leveraged existing policies, especially in agriculture and for basic\nnecessities other than fuels. The authorities underscored that the main goal of these policies was to\nsupport historically underserved agricultural communities, although they also expected positive\neffects on agricultural yields.\n\nB. Managing Potential Downside Risks\n\nFiscal Policy\n\n28.     Fiscal policy could better prepare for downside risks. The neutral fiscal stance could be\nmaintained if growth were to slow modestly. However, more could be done to be ready to provide\ntargeted support for poorer households. In the event of a significant weakening of activity,\nadditional discretionary fiscal support should be considered.\n\n29.    Under Mexico’s fiscal framework—which includes both a balanced budget rule and\nconstraints on debt issuance—the scope to pursue a countercyclical fiscal policy is limited.\nModest steps could, however, increase the ability of fiscal policy to provide targeted support in the\nevent of a negative shock.\n\n•    Changing the retail fuel price regime could create room for more targeted support in case\n     of a prolonged global oil price increase. More market-based fuel pricing could allow for a\n     passthrough of global fuel prices to domestic retail prices, encourage transition to greener\n     sources of energy, and reduce the budgetary cost of subsidies. This would also strengthen the\n     price signals for fuel demand and allow the fiscal savings to be, at least partially, reinvested in\n     targeted support for vulnerable households 7 by leveraging existing social safety nets (see Fiscal\n     Policy for Mitigating the Social Impact of High Energy and Food Prices, IMF 2022) or relying on\n\n7 The fuel tax expenditure under the current regime entails large leakage of support to higher income households.\n\nUsing 2020 data, the authorities estimate that the top 20 percent of the income distribution accounted for about 45\npercent of the fuel excise tax collection whereas the bottom 20 percent accounted for 6 percent (Distribución del\npago de impuestos y recepción del gasto público por deciles de hogares y personas. Resultados para el año 2020,\nMinistry of Finance). Broad-based reductions in tax rates are therefore likely to entail disproportionately large gains\nfor richer households.\n\n\n16    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": "947592e6ac6b78daff5a5827380af57b6c1edf17bf36fddbad6e2a1f49a252f8", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports successive, gradually larger rate increases; an appropriately calibrated response; a restrictive stance; strengthened communication; and significant uncertainty with upside inflation risks."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E003", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "Fiscal reserves in Mexico’s stabilization fund had fallen below 0.1 percent of GDP after extensive pandemic use. Fiscal reserves\n    in the Fondo de Estabilización de los Ingresos Presupuestarios (FEIP, also known as ‘the\n    stabilization fund’) have fallen to less than 0.1 percent of GDP, given extensive usage during the\n    pandemic and a restrictive replenishment mechanism under the Federal Budget and Fiscal\n    Responsibility Law (FRBL). 8 This is a potential constraint on the government’s ability to respond\n    quickly to future shocks. The authorities estimate that a minimum of 0.3 percent of GDP would\n    provide sufficient resources for a fiscal policy response. Recent amendments to the FRBL\n    proposed by the authorities as part of the 2023 budget would bring FEIP reserves closer to this\n    desired level, by enabling utilization of headroom against the government’s debt ceiling. This\n    approach would enhance preparedness in the short-term. However, in the medium term, more\n    extensive reforms are required to enhance flexibility more permanently, while also ensuring\n    sustainability and credibility over the medium-term (Annex X). Such reforms could include\n    (Annex IV of Staff Report 2019): (i) a well-calibrated debt anchor; (ii) broader coverage of\n    expenditure in the structural spending rule; (iii) a medium-term fiscal strategy that specifies the\n    post-shock adjustment path to return to the debt target; (iv) tighter triggers for the use of\n    escape clauses; and (v) a strengthened role for the fiscal council.\n\n30.     A medium-term fiscal strategy could help to anchor expectations about future fiscal\npolicy. The public debt ratio is expected to remain broadly unchanged in the next decade. 9 Weaker-\nthan-expected growth or adjustment slippages would thus result in a rising debt path. Nonetheless,\nthe Sovereign Risk and Debt Sustainability Analysis (SRDSA) (Annex III) indicates that public debt\nremains sustainable. With the proposed fiscal framework reform, a credible debt anchor and a\nmedium-term fiscal strategy could increase the scope to let fiscal policy act as a shock absorber and\nreduce the volatility in activity as a result.\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n31.     The authorities see advantages to maintaining the current retail fuel price stabilization\nmechanism. Given the current administration’s pledge that fuel prices will not rise in real terms\nabove their November 2018 level and the legal basis of the pricing mechanism, the authorities\nprefer to continue with the current prompt and programmatic fuel price stabilization mechanism\nrather than more targeted support.\n\n8 Under Mexico’s fiscal framework, FEIP has served as the de facto source of contingent financing for the budget\n\nalongside smaller trust funds when revenue shortfalls materialized during the 2020 downturn. However, in anticipation of possible downside risks, they are interested in reforms to\nthe fiscal framework that would enable the allocation of fiscal savings and funding from debt\nissuance to the stabilization fund, while respecting key tenets of the current framework. The Subcontracting Law, Employment, and GDP\n                             Measurement\nThe authorities have passed a new legislation restricting the subcontracting of the workforce in 2021.\nThis annex reports that a large share of workers who used to be subcontracted transitioned to direct\nemployment status. This has triggered a large decline in the production of those services sectors\nproviding support to business. However, this may not reflect a reduction of activity as part of the value\nadded that used to be generated in these sectors is now generated in the sectors employing workers\ndirectly.\n\n1.      Labor subcontracting in Mexico grew rapidly in the past two decades, from 6 percent\nof total employment in 2003 to 15 percent in 2018 in Mexico. According to the 2019 economic\ncensus, subcontracted workers accounted for more than 40 percent of employment in utilities and\nfor 36 percent in financial services.\n\n2.       In April 2021, the authorities enacted new legislation restricting the subcontracting of\nthe workforce, with the aim of preventing the violation of labor law and tax evasion. The new\nlaw bans service firms from providing employees to be supervised and managed by a third-party,\nwhile it still allows subcontracting of services if the employees are monitored by the firm providing\nthe service. 1 The legislation also tightened registration and reporting requirements. Domestic Market Development, Foreign Investors and\n                        Peso Stability\nDespite continued portfolio outflows and tightening global financial conditions, the peso has been one\nof the better performing currencies over the past year, even strengthening versus the dollar. This annex\nlooks at some of the drivers of the recent performance, including the importance of the increasing rate\ndifferential to the U.S. Further, with foreigners continuing to reduce their holdings of peso-\ndenominated government bonds, the annex considers the impact of pension funds in the market. With\nthe recent pension reforms, these funds are expected to play an increasingly important role.\n\n1.      Foreigners (nonresident investors) have been reducing their holdings of domestic,\npeso-denominated government securities since early 2015. The decline was recently in part\ndriven by China’s entry to the global bond index and a consequent loss of Mexico’s weight. While\ngenerally gradual, the reductions temporarily accelerated during periods of increasing risk aversion,\nincluding in the COVID-19 pandemic. Despite the implied capital outflows, the peso and local-\ncurrency government securities markets have remained relatively stable.\n\nA. Peso Stability\n\n2. Fiscal Policies to Tackle the Effects of Inflation in\n                                     Mexico\nThis annex considers the effects of rising fuel and food on households in Mexico and government\npolicies to cushion their impact. Poverty – officially measured using the cost of a basic basket of\nnecessities as a poverty line – has showed modest improvements since its peak in 2020 but rising\nprices are exerting pressure on the poor. The government’s fuel pricing mechanism has helped to\nrestrain the effect of fuel price volatility on headline inflation in 2022 but only by subsuming the\nsizable oil revenue gains that Mexico has accrued as an oil exporter. Measures to tackle rising food\nprices have leveraged and scaled up, albeit to a limited extent, pre-existing social and agricultural\nsupport programs. In addition, both public and private sectors have taken steps to limit the pass-\nthrough of high prices to consumers, though the impact is not clear.\n\n1.      Rising prices and other essential items have put further strains on the poor on the\nheels of an increase in poverty since the pandemic. By the end of 2020, rural poverty had posted\na modest improvement, with around 56.8 percent of the rural population in poverty or extreme\npoverty compared to 57.7 percent in 2018. However, the same period also saw a deterioration of\nconditions in urban areas, with around 40.1 percent of urban households estimated to be in some\nform of poverty—up from 36.8 percent in 2018—as illness and public health restrictions affected the\neconomy (CONEVAL, 2022). Despite signs of a modest recovery, rising inflation strained real\nincomes in 2021, with price pressures from processed foods, including imported ones, a key\ncontributor (IMF, 2021). Since then, rising food price inflation has continued to exert pressure on the\npoor, with the price of the minimum food consumption basket—the minimum food basket that a\nhousehold should be able to consume to ensure adequate nutrition and the extreme poverty line\nused in Mexico—rising by close to 2 percent for both rural and urban households between March\nand June of this year.\n\n                   Gasoline Prices 1/                                                            Evolution of Basic Food Basket Costs 2/                                              Cost of Basic Food Basket as Share of Per\n            (Pesos per liter, monthly average)                                        125                                                                                                     Capita Labor Income 3/ 4/\n 30                                                                                                                                                                                 100\n\n                  U.S. Gulf coast                                                                                                                                                   90\n 25               Mexico retail price (incl. The authorities have launched a wide-ranging package of measures to tackle the\nimpact of rising inflation on households in 2022, with fuel subsidies front-and-center. As the\neffects of rising inflation began to hit households in early 2022, the Mexican government launched a\npackage of measures to mitigate the impact under the name Paquete Contra La Inflación y La\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND                                                     69\n\nMEXICO\n\n\nCarestía (PACIC) in May. The affordability of fuel     Gasoline subsidy                                                  395,400                   1.36\nsubsidies in 2022 ultimately           Total                                                              541,223                  1.86\nhinges on the relative prices of       Source: SHCP.\n\nthe unrefined oil that Mexico          1/ Includes freezing of toll rates and the reduction in import tariffs on basic foodstuffs.\n\nexports and the refined oil that it imports. As an exporter of unrefined oil, Mexico has benefitted\nfrom an increase in oil revenues due to rising oil prices at the same time that it has subsidized the\nhigher cost of fuel for domestic consumers, who largely consume refined oil product imports. While\ndomestic fuel subsidies are expected to cost MEX$395.4 billion, the windfall gain in oil revenues\nrelative to the 2022 budget approved in 2021 is estimated at MEX$397.8 billion, suggesting that oil\nexports can cover the costs of the subsidy scheme. However, if there were another global fuel price\nspike with sharply higher refining margins (“crack spread”), the cost of the fuel subsidy could be\nlarger than the increase in oil revenue. Maintaining a small net impact would then require some\nsubstantial expenditure adjustment in other areas.\n\n4.       Despite large costs in 2022, the fuel pricing mechanism will ensure that subsidies\ndecline with market prices and subsidies are expected to be substantially smaller in 2023.\nSubsidies themselves are enshrined in the law by Presidential decree and their size is currently\ndetermined by a methodology outlined in 2019. 1 Under this methodology, shadow prices for\ndifferent fuels are constructed. Where the actual fuel price exceeds that of the shadow price, the\nrelevant excise rate is adjusted downwards to close the gap. Shadow prices are constructed using\nfour components. First, a base price component is set at the fuel price as of November 2018,\nuprated with inflation every 15 days. A second component is a reference price that tracks\ncontemporaneous trends in commodity markets - this is based on the U.S. Gulf Coast waterborne\nticker. Finally, a transport cost component tracks trends in the cost of transporting fuel and a tax\ncomponent is added to control for taxation. All-in-all, the formula aims to ensure that shadow prices\ntrack relevant trends in the cost of fuel while also attempting to smooth excess upward cost\npressures. Given projected declines in global oil prices and inflation in 2023, shadow prices and\n\n\n1 See https://www.dof.gob.mx/nota_detalle.php?codigo=5583176&fecha=31/12/2019#gsc.tab=0 for the fuel pricing\n\nformula. The Presidential decree establishes that the statutory excise rate be suspended and fuel subsidies be\nimplemented where actual fuel prices exceed the shadow price.\n\n\n\n70    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\nactual prices are expected to broadly converge, drastically reducing subsidies next year. Moreover,\npast experience evidences the authorities’ willingness to remove subsidies in this situation.\n5.       Fuel subsidies have helped to contain inflation in 2022. As global fuel prices rose after\nRussia’s invasion of Ukraine, subsidies substantially contained retail fuel prices. Peaking in June,\nsubsidies reduced prices by as much as 30 percent. With fuel at about 6 percent of the consumption\nbasket, the direct effects of the fuel subsidy reduced headline inflation by about two percentage\npoints, when it would have peaked at nearly 10 percent. This estimate of the impact of subsidies on\ninflation may also be a lower bound given second round effects, for example the effect of lower fuel\nprices on retail goods for which distribution is a significant production cost, or public transport costs\nwhere many cities have flexible pricing that responds to fuel prices. However, falling fuel prices since\nthe early summer have reduced the effect of subsidies on inflation, with their effect now estimated\nat a modest 0.2 percent in direct contribution to containing inflation.\n\n6.       Meanwhile, the Mexican government has sought to leverage pre-existing supply-side\npolicies funded by small budget reallocations to tackle food price inflation. Launched in 2019,\nthe Sembrando Vida policy is aimed at addressing rural poverty, tackling environmental degradation,\nand reducing Mexico’s dependence on imported foods by providing financial incentives to\nintroduce more sustainable and higher-yielding farming techniques. Producción para el Bienestar\nfollows a similar model but for small and medium grain producers while price guarantees for\nproducers of some products and delivery of fertilizer to small-scale farmers in specific states have\naimed at providing direct support to the agriculture sector. As the impact of shortages in\nagricultural inputs have continued to mount, the government has scaled up some of these schemes,\nextending the fertilizer program to nine states from five and also introducing an emergency fertilizer\nproduction program. Pre-existing policies aimed at households have also received greater attention\nduring this period of rising food prices, including the Liconsa milk subsidy for households below the\nextreme poverty line and the provision of subsidized food at Diconsa stores, a network of\ncommunity-owned stores supported by the government. The focus on pre-existing policies has\nprovided the government with instruments to react relatively quickly to price pressures; however,\nthe scale of these policies is somewhat limited at around 0.3 percent of GDP. Moreover, a lack of\nclarity on policy objectives may serve to hinder efficacy. For example, the objective of Sembrando\nVida appears to be to both reduce rural poverty and reduce inflation by boosting food supplies.\nHowever, the requirement that recipients demonstrate land ownership likely excludes the poorest\nwhile focusing on smaller farmers may not represent the best way of increasing food supply.\n\n7.      Newer policies have attempted to limit the pass-through of higher prices to\nconsumers directly but with unclear impact. In May, the President announced that a number of\nmajor retailers in Mexico had agreed to restrain price increases for 24 staple goods initially for six\nmonths with no fiscal or regulatory implications for the government. Additionally, the government\nalso announced a reduction in tariffs for imports of 21 basic goods and five inputs for food\nproduction. Given domestic production of a number of these goods and the exemption from tariffs\nof imports under free trade agreements, the cost of this policy in terms of revenues foregone is\nexpected to be small—around MEX$1.2 billion in 2022.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND        71", "excerpt_sha256": "935cce5ddf9bcb275bff705e5ebbe2ae2bec9f461cbafdeec37d49314093505a", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports PACIC’s fuel-subsidy focus, the use of pre-existing agricultural and food programs, substantial inflation reduction from fuel subsidies, their budgetary cost and limited scale, and unclear effects from retailer agreements."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0022-R02-E001", "source_id": "SRC-5ab6ce418229e78d", "url": "https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/099345310142441749/pdf/IDU1f65e654e15bce1426818068130b6ab9c31e7.pdf", "title": "Macro Poverty Outlook: Mexico", "publisher": "World Bank", "raw_sha256": "d976901f9fab417fb7468399818c8847d4bc95a6f1c32cf073466e005d50b0ec", "fact_summary": "Mexico’s real GDP grew 3.2% in 2023; official multidimensional poverty fell from 43.2% in 2016 to 36.3% in 2022, lifting 5.4 million people out of poverty. the review identified 32 corruption cases for 2023 and alleged selective protection of close collaborators and insufficient accountability.", "question_ids": ["5B.5", "7B.1", "7B.10", "7B.2", "7B.4", "7B.6", "7B.7", "7B.9", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "Directly addresses the 2023 corruption record and the president's response to evidence available during his term.", "ruler_attribution": "The source directly attributes the public statement and protective posture to López Obrador; the documented procurement warnings and cases are attributed to investigative organizations and media.", "limitations": ["The extract summarizes reported cases and does not provide final judicial findings for each case.", "The claim of selective protection is the source's analytical conclusion."], "start_unit": 31, "end_unit": 32, "locator": "extracted HTML block 31 through extracted HTML block 32", "exact_excerpt": "4dm1n\nHan pasado más de dos años desde que López Obrador anunció quiénes podrían ser los candidatos de su partido a la presidencia Desde entonces, el país se ha visto envuelto en la campaña más adelantada de la historia. Ante la elección que arranca en veinte días, es útil tener en cuenta una serie de datos duros.\nPublicado en:\nSólo en línea\n2023: la corrupción campante\n1 febrero, 2024\n2 marzo, 2024\n4dm1n\nEl presidente Andrés Manuel López Obrador aseguró que el desvío de más de 15 000 millones de pesos del presupuesto de Seguridad Alimentaria Mexicana —organismo sectorizado a la Secretaría de Agricultura y Desarrollo Rural, conocido como Segalmex— es “el único caso que hemos tenido de corrupción” en el sexenio. Los medios reportaron como noticia que el presidente reconocía, por fin, un acto de corrupción relacionado con su gobierno. El problema: esto sucedió hasta diciembre de 2023 y, por supuesto, él no tuvo conocimiento de tal desfalco; a pesar de haber repetido una y otra vez que “todos los actos de corrupción, los más dañinos, y todos los negocios que se realizan al amparo del poder público, llevan siempre el visto bueno del presidente de la República”.\nLa primera vez que se encendieron las alarmas sobre las irregularidades de Segalmex fue tan pronto como abril de 2019, cuando el análisis de compras públicas de Mexicanos Contra la Corrupción y la Impunidad (MCCI) destacó problemas en las contrataciones de esa institución. Para 2020, los primeros desvíos ya habían sido ampliamente documentados por la Unidad de Periodismo y figuraron en el Anuario de la Corrupción de ese año. MCCI, así como otros medios de comunicación y organizaciones, documentaron más y más desvíos que se fueron apilando a lo largo de los años. De millón en millón, Diconsa, Liconsa y Segalmex acumularon desvíos de las dimensiones que hoy conocemos, y año con año cada nuevo\nmodus operandi\nse reportó en las entregas subsecuentes del Anuario de la Corrupción.\nLa corrupción en Segalmex no es el único caso de desvío de recursos, tráfico de influencias, uso ilegal del dinero público y otros delitos dentro de la administración de López Obrador y de los gobiernos de su partido o sus aliados. Cada entrega de nuestro anuario —con su recuento detallado de los principales casos de corrupción en el transcurso de cada año y sus consecuencias en el ámbito político y en el sistema de justicia— nos muestra los casos de corrupción que se siguen acumulando en México.\nLas declaraciones del presidente no son inocuas. Aparejada a su palabra y a la defensa a ultranza de todos sus colaboradores y personas cercanas, hay una justicia que se aplica de manera selectiva: todo aquello que en su gobierno no se considera como corrupción es protegido con un halo de impunidad. Por ello, es fundamental para la memoria pública este seguimiento que da cuenta de los principales casos de corrupción documentados y que deben preocupar a la ciudadanía. Para 2023 identificamos 32 casos; la mayoría corresponde al gobierno federal y a los gobiernos de la coalición gobernante, aunque también hay casos en el resto de partidos y poderes.\nA unos cuantos meses de que termine el sexenio del presidente López Obrador, este anuario registra un año más en que el combate a la corrupción se mantuvo sin cambios: muchas palabras pero pocos resultados.\nPublicado en:\n2024 Febrero\n,\nAgenda\nLa intervención militar en el gobierno civil\n1 febrero, 2024\n27 enero, 2024\n4dm1n\nPublicado en:\n2024 Febrero\n\n,\nExpediente\nLa batalla por la Suprema Corte\n2019-2023\n1 febrero, 2024\n1 mayo, 2024\n4dm1n\nEl obradorismo lanzó un asalto de dimensiones nunca vistas contra el Poder Judicial y algunos órganos constitucionales autónomos. Los ataques han sido constantes y variados: desde iniciativas y reformas a la Constitución para desaparecerlos o mutilarlos hasta descalificaciones contra sus integrantes, pasando por reducir el presupuesto, denuncias penales, amenazas de juicio político y designaciones que siguen la lógica de la captura partidista. No ha sido un sexenio pacífico para los órganos de contrapeso.\nEstas instituciones han respondido de modos distintos. Algunas han resistido. Otras han claudicado. La Comisión Nacional de los Derechos Humanos (CNDH), después de la cuestionable designación de Rosario Piedra Ibarra, claudicó. Basta con leer sus comunicados para constatar cómo una institución, que en momentos jugó un papel relevante en la defensa de los derechos, renunció a ser un contrapeso para convertirse en una oficina de propaganda gubernamental.\nOtras instituciones decidieron defender de manera abierta y firme su autonomía. Quizá el mejor ejemplo sea el Instituto Nacional Electoral (INE), sobre todo durante la presidencia de Lorenzo Córdova. En este periodo, el INE se defendió tanto en la palabra como en los hechos y no se abstuvo de denunciar y controvertir judicialmente los intentos por desmantelar el sistema electoral mexicano. Ésa es también la estrategia que ha seguido la Suprema Corte de Justicia de la Nación (SCJN) después de la elección de la presidenta Norma Piña.\nSe ha dicho, sin embargo, que existe una tercera vía. Según algunos renombrados comentaristas, es la estrategia que siguió la Suprema Corte durante la presidencia de Arturo Zaldívar. En vez de confrontar al presidente López Obrador, las autonomías tenían la oportunidad, en palabras de Ana Laura Magaloni, de “acordar” con el presidente, de “convencerlo” de sus errores e, incluso, de “decirle que no” en temas políticamente relevantes.\n1\nAl comentar la decisión de la Suprema Corte sobre la consulta popular, Enrique Quintana sintetiza la lógica de esta supuesta tercera vía: “La posibilidad de que se pueda eludir el enfrentamiento con el presidente sin que tenga que haber sometimiento”.\n2\nAquí analizo el desempeño del tribunal constitucional mexicano en el sistema de pesos y contrapesos durante las presidencias de Zaldívar y Piña. Entre otras cosas, me interesa explicar y aquilatar los beneficios y los costos de tal estrategia. ¿Fue posible que en el periodo de Zaldívar la Corte evitara la confrontación y evitara ser sumisa ante López Obrador? ¿Cuáles fueron las causas y los costos de la estrategia de Zaldívar? ¿Ha cambiado la lógica de la Corte durante la presidencia de Piña? ¿Cuáles son los riesgos y las oportunidades de tal apuesta? ¿Cuáles son las implicaciones para el sistema de pesos y contrapesos?\nLo que me interesa mostrar es que la contención provocó que la Corte claudicara. Lejos de ser un intermediario, Zaldívar utilizó su cargo para beneficiarse personalmente y lucrar en términos políticos. Fungió como correa de transmisión de los deseos presidenciales para hacerse un lugar en la porra del obradorismo. Bajo esta mirada, la renuncia temprana de Zaldívar es sólo un paso más en la carrera de un político que durante catorce años vistió la toga judicial. Tal estrategia le rindió indiscutibles frutos a Zaldívar, pero el costo para la Corte fue altísimo: se cuestionó su legitimidad por decisiones apartadas de la norma jurídica y se generaron vacantes inconstitucionales que le dieron una mayor influencia a López Obrador en la configuración de la Corte. Al contrario, durante la era de Piña una mayoría de ministras y ministros ha sustituido el colaboracionismo por la defensa frontal de la independencia judicial. La estrategia tiene costos pero parece que, poco a poco, la Corte ha corregido el rumbo.\nPublicado en:\n2024 Febrero\n,\nEnsayo\nEl villismo y la violencia hoy\n1 febrero, 2024\n27 enero, 2024\n4dm1n\nPublicado en:", "excerpt_sha256": "aa2907de3395af71d7e565d8e10902fb4812867d661bc1ba8f816d3ec9c1d848", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0030-E004", "source_id": "SRC-45ecea1b6d03e5ea", "url": "https://apnews.com/article/7b0db1b6b262acde6721debceaa5af7e", "title": "Maya Train Partial Opening", "publisher": "Associated Press", "raw_sha256": "0f5e31b7c068f009b871586614703da31ecc20907a910c59933dc9c454170a36", "fact_summary": "In December 2023 López Obrador inaugurated only a partially completed 290-mile section of the Maya Train. (AP Photo/Martin Zetina)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\n\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nUpdated [hour]:[minute] [AMPM] [timezone], [monthFull] [day], [year]\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\n\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nMEXICO CITY (AP) — Mexico’s Maya Train rail project opened partially to the general public Saturday, amid hours-long delays and huge cost overruns.\nPassengers waiting for the twice-daily train to leave the resort of Cancun were left waiting on the platform for about five hours before being able to board. Officials apologized for the delayed and said it was due to trains being “reconfigured.”\nSome passengers napped on the floor of the concrete platform. Some — many self-declared supporters of President Andrés Manuel López Obrador — cheered when the train finally appeared in Cancun.\nThe train running in the other direction from the Gulf coast colonial city of Campeche was also delayed for hours, because only one side of the planned double rail line is finished. Officials estimated it would take about 5 1/2 hours to travel the 290 miles (473 kilometers) from Campeche to Cancun.\nRelated Coverage\nMexico’s president inaugurates first part of $20 billion tourist train project on Yucatan peninsula\nWorries abound that Mexico’s Maya Train will destroy jungle\nMexican officials admit secrecy-shrouded border train project had no environmental impact study\nMeanwhile, the cost of the project has soared from original estimates of around $8.5 billion, to as much as $28 billion.\nThe 950-mile line,\ncalled the Maya Train\n, is meant to connect beach resorts and archaeological sites. However, only about one-third of it — the 290-mile (473-kilometer) stretch that President Andrés Manuel López Obrador inaugurated with dignitaries and the press Friday — has even been partly finished.\nOfficials pledged the rest of the line would be ready by the end of February.\nBut even on the part López Obrador inaugurated Friday\n, just a single line of a planned double-line track has been finished, meaning one train has to wait on a siding while another passes.\nThe stretch running between Campeche and Cancun is about one-third of the entire project and covers the least controversial portion of the route, which crosses many environmentally sensitive areas.\nRead More\nA first-class ticket on one of the two trains from Cancun to Merida, the most popular stop, will cost the equivalent of $68. A first-class bus covers the route in about the same time and costs about $58, with buses leaving about every half hour from the city centers, rather than remote train stations on the outskirts.\nThat led to questions about whether the train will ever cover its operating costs, much less its construction budget.\nMexico’s army, which operates the train and built part of the railway, did not respond to a request for comment about the delays or cost overruns.\n\nSign up for Morning Wire:\nOur flagship newsletter breaks down the biggest headlines of the day.\nEmail address\nSign up\nBy checking this box, you agree to AP's\nTerms of Use\nand acknowledge that AP may collect and use your data pursuant to our\nPrivacy Policy\n.\nIn comments Friday, López Obrador acknowledged, “Yes, things are lacking (on the train), of course,” and predicted it would take “three years, four years” for the train system to begin covering its operating costs under the best scenario.\nAsked how much the construction cost of the project would be, the president said: “I don’t know ... I don’t have the exact figure.”\nOriginally projected to cost $8.6 billion, by now $22.7 billion has been assigned to the project, and Treasury Secretary Rogelio Ramírez told local media the final cost would be around $28 billion.\nUnlike the remaining two-thirds of the Maya Train, the part of the line inaugurated Friday already had an old train line running over much of the route. Many of the still-unfinished parts were cut through the jungle and built over sensitive, relic-filled cave systems, drawing objections from environmentalists.\nLópez Obrador has raced to finish the Maya Train project before he leaves office in September, rolling over the objections of ecologists, cave divers and archaeologists.\nThe train runs along the Caribbean coast and\nthreatens extensive caves\nwhere some of the oldest human remains in North America have been discovered. Because of the region’s limestone geology, it is riddled with caves that carry most of its water.\nWhile officials have touted the train as utilitarian transport for freight and local residents, its only real source of significant income would be tourists. However, given its frequent stops, unwieldy route and lack of feasibility studies, it is unclear how many tourists will actually want to buy tickets.\n“The train won’t help residents get to work or school, and besides, it’s very expensive,” SELVAME, a coalition of groups opposing the project, said in a statement Friday. “The train runs through the jungle, filling cenotes (sinkhole lakes) and underground rivers with concrete, without any studies.”\nLópez Obrador has tried to rush through the\nproject\nby exempting it from normal permitting, public reporting and environmental impact statements, claiming it is vital to national security.\nIn November 2021, his administration issued a broad decree requiring all federal agencies to give automatic approval for any public works project the government deems to be “in the national interest” or to “involve national security.”\n____\nFollow AP’s coverage of Latin America and the Caribbean at", "excerpt_sha256": "12befd1f0d10756729f3909a1366f54049d2c7b9d8d9d4697a88e6ecd075e4ca", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0032-E001", "source_id": "SRC-cc6454689011bcb0", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Pobreza_Multidimensional_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "1251448088af9b7a6b3d92bac9efb6a57f7afbe3e62304bbf9e6499b567abe5c", "fact_summary": "Entre 2018 y 2022, el PIB mexicano se recuperó tras la contracción pandémica, pero el PIB y el PIB per cápita de 2022 permanecieron por debajo de los niveles de 2018; el ingreso laboral aumentó y la población ocupada creció en 3.9 millones. El periodo de 2018 a 2022 contó con\ndiversos panoramas, entre los cuales se presentó la pandemia por COVID-19 y el confinamiento,\nel conflicto en Europa oriental, la pérdida de ingresos de los hogares, el incremento de la deuda\npública, entre otros.4 El presente apartado, integra algunos de los elementos más relevantes para\ncomplementar el análisis de la pobreza multidimensional.\n\n\n\nProducto Interno Bruto\n\nDesde 2018 se observa un descenso en la actividad económica nacional. Datos trimestrales del Pro-\nducto Interno Bruto (PIB), reportados por el Inegi, permiten dar cuenta de la desaceleración econó-\nmica durante el periodo. En los primeros trimestres de 2019 la economía mexicana presentó una con-\ntracción generalizada de la actividad económica (Inegi, 2023c). En 2020, la situación económica del\npaís tuvo una caída en el segundo trimestre con la llegada de la pandemia por COVID-19 (CONEVAL,\n2022b), que se tradujo en una reducción del PIB en más de diez puntos porcentuales (gráfica 1).\n\n\n\n\n4\n    Para mayor referencia, véase el documento de análisis sobre la medición multidimensional de la pobreza, 2022 y la nota técnica sobre la información empleada para la\n    estimación de la medición de pobreza en México, 2022 (Anexo I).\n\n\n\n\n     20           Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022\n\nGráfica 1. Aunque tanto el PIB como\nel PIB per cápita permitieron observar una recuperación económica durante los primeros tres trimestres\nde 2022, dinámica que se mantuvo por debajo de lo observado en 2018 (CONEVAL, 2023a).\n\n\n\nIngreso laboral subordinado\n\nEn lo que corresponde al ingreso laboral de las personas que desempeñaban una ocupación subor-\ndinada, este tuvo una participación de más de la mitad del ingreso corriente total per cápita (ICTPC)\ntanto en el 2018 como en 2022. Para 2022 representó 61.5 % del ingreso total de los hogares, mientras\nque en 2018 fue de 62.8 %. A pesar del cambio en la participación, el ingreso total fue mayor para 2022.\n\n\nEl análisis de las remuneraciones permite observar un incremento en el monto de aquellas obtenidas\npor medio del trabajo subordinado, de acuerdo con la formalidad de la ocupación y del ámbito de\nresidencia.5 Aunque el aumento en el salario mensual promedio en el sector informal fue mayor que\nen el formal, este último presentó un acrecentamiento considerable en el salario mínimo desde el\n2000 a la fecha, el cual fue mayor en la Zona Libre de la Frontera Norte (12.7 %) con respecto al resto\ndel país (6.8 %) (CONEVAL, 2023a).\n\n5\n    Los resultados de un análisis más profundo acerca de este tema se muestran en apartados posteriores.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                 Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social            21\n\nAdicional al incremento en el ingreso laboral, el porcentaje de población ocupada aumentó en 3.9\nmillones de personas de 2018 a 2022. Por su parte, el número de hogares creció en 9.2 % entre 2018 y\n2022. En el mismo periodo se registró una reducción mínima, pero estadísticamente significativa, en\nel número de personas promedio que perciben ingreso laboral en los hogares a nivel nacional de\n1.72 a 1.66 personas.\n\n\n\nReforma laboral 2021\n\nEn 2021 fue promulgada la Reforma a la Ley Federal del Trabajo (DOF, 2021a), la cual modifica los\nartículos 12 a 15 y establece la prohibición de la subcontratación del empleo para el desarrollo de\nactividades esenciales relacionadas con el objeto social de las empresas, y se conforma el padrón\nde personas físicas o morales que realicen servicios de subcontratación especializados. La reforma\nbusca promover la formalidad laboral y mejores condiciones para las personas trabajadoras. Al\ntransitar a la formalidad laboral, la población empleada podrá acceder a prestaciones tales como\nla seguridad social, reparto de utilidades, reconocimiento de antigüedad, entre otros (STPS, 2021).\n\n\nAl analizar el ingreso de la población en función del tipo de empleo, formal o informal,6 se observa\nun patrón relacional con respecto a cada uno de los deciles del ingreso corriente total per cápita.\nMientras que los hogares de los primeros deciles cuentan con mayor proporción de su población\ninmersa en empleos informales, aquellas que se encuentran en los últimos deciles presentan mayor\nproporción de personas con empleos formales (gráfica 2).\n\n\nEn este sentido, para 2022, por cada 100 perceptores con ingreso laboral, en el decil I, 4 de estos\ncontaban con trabajos formales y el resto informales. En tanto que, en el caso de los hogares del\ndecil X, esta relación es de 62 empleos formales de cada 100. Adicionalmente, en todos los deciles\nse puede observar una ligera reducción en el indicador de informalidad entre 2018 y 2022, sin em-\nbargo, la proporción de población ocupada en el sector informal es mayor que la empleada bajo\nun esquema formal.\n\n\n\n\n6\n    Se considera empleo formal el que dota a las personas empleadas, en su trabajo principal, de acceso a servicios de salud. Por el contrario, se denominan como informales a\n    aquellas personas que por su trabajo principal no tienen acceso a servicios de salud (CONEVAL, 2023a).\n\n\n\n\n     22            Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022\n\nGráfica 2. política social experimentó una reestructuración tanto en términos\npresupuestales como a nivel programático. En 2018, el presupuesto ejercido en programas y accio-\nnes federales de desarrollo social fue de 934,595.1 millones de pesos destinados a un total de 150\nprogramas. En 2022, el presupuesto ejercido alcanzó la cifra de 1,135,232.7 millones de pesos, desti-\nnados a un total de 121 programas (CONEVAL, 2023e).7 El presupuesto para la política de desarrollo\nsocial de 2022 representó el 4.7 % del PIB; mientras que para 2018 este gasto entonces representaba\nel 3.9 % del PIB.\n\n\n\n\n7\n    Montos de acuerdo con el valor de pesos constantes de 2018.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                             Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social          23", "excerpt_sha256": "b9b5b5bbcab87912bb646003f467716c1080b21bad718a1dc17b7cd2579ac354", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0032-E002", "source_id": "SRC-cc6454689011bcb0", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Pobreza_Multidimensional_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "1251448088af9b7a6b3d92bac9efb6a57f7afbe3e62304bbf9e6499b567abe5c", "fact_summary": "La pobreza multidimensional disminuyó de 41.9% de la población en 2018 a 36.3% en 2022, pero la pobreza extrema aumentó en términos absolutos de 8.7 a 9.1 millones de personas. La población vulnerable por carencias sociales aumentó de 26.4% a 29.4%, y la población con al menos tres carencias sociales aumentó de 20.2% a 24.9%. De esta población, el\n29.3 % presentó pobreza moderada y un 7.1 % pobreza extrema. Esta última, si bien en términos relativos\npresentó cifras similares a 2018, en términos absolutos pasó de 8.7 a 9.1 millones de personas.\n\n\nCuadro 1. Para 2022, la ausencia de población en situación de pobreza en\nel sexto decil podría estar relacionada con la participación del ingreso por transferencias monetarias\ngubernamentales y con el incremento en el ingreso laboral.\n\n\nEn lo que refiere a la pobreza extrema en ambos años de análisis se distribuyó entre los dos primeros\ndeciles; sin embargo, en 2022, se presentó una mayor concentración en el decil I. Esto indica que las\ntransferencias monetarias por programas sociales cuentan con una participación importante entre la\npoblación del decil I; sin embargo, los apoyos monetarios no son suficientes para revertir esta situación,\ndadas las profundas dificultades en cuanto al acceso a los derechos sociales presentes en este sector.\n\n\nGráfica 3. De acuerdo con la Ley General de Desarrollo So-\ncial (2022), el espacio del bienestar económico se expresa por medio del ingreso corriente total per\ncápita, mientras que el de los derechos sociales está representado por las carencias por acceso a\nlos derechos de educación, salud, alimentación nutritiva y de calidad, vivienda y seguridad social.\n\n\nAl analizar los resultados de la pobreza multidimensional 2022 y compararlos con los de 2018, sobre-\nsalen las diferencias con respecto a la incidencia de las carencias sociales en la población. Así, a\nnivel nacional, la población que presenta una o más carencias sociales, pero cuyo ingreso es su-\nperior a la línea de pobreza por ingresos, es decir, la población vulnerable por carencias, pasó de\n32.7 a 37.9 millones de personas (26.4 % a 29.4 %, respectivamente). Esto supone que el aumento en el\ningreso de los hogares no se tradujo en una reducción de sus carencias promedio.\n\n\n\n                                                                                             Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   25\n\nEl dato anterior refleja que el acceso a los derechos sociales sigue siendo un reto pendiente para la\npolítica pública de desarrollo social, la cual requiere de un avance integral en las dimensiones de la\npobreza. Al observar la suma de carencias que presenta cada persona (índice de privación social),\nse identificó que en 2018 el 68.3 % de la población (84.6 millones de personas) presentó al menos una\ncarencia social y el 20.2 % de la población (25.0 millones de personas) presentó al menos 3 carencias\nsociales. En 2022, esta situación fue de 65.7 %, (84.7 millones de personas) para la población con al\nmenos una carencia y en aquellas con al menos 3 carencias pasó a 24.9 % (32.1 millones de personas).\n\n\nAl interior de cada decil de acuerdo con el ingreso corriente total per cápita, las carencias sociales\nse encuentran presentes en cada uno de estos segmentos de población. Esto deja en evidencia\nque, a menores ingresos en el hogar, mayor es el número de carencias sociales y, por el contrario, a\nmayor ingreso, menor es la presencia de carencias (gráfica 4).\n\n\nGráfica 4.
